# Development log


What this fork has landed, and what was decided while landing it. Newest first.

This is the development half of the board. The other half — `1 · How the pieces fit`,
`2 · The blocks on the canvas`, `3 · Try it` — is the **Project structure** map: what the tool
is made of, right now, undated. This one is the record of how it got that way, and every row
carries the date it landed on.

It replaces `delivered.md` and `open-work.md`, which said the same thing about a smaller
repository and then stopped. `delivered.md` still opened with "Issues #1–#11, all closed" when
`main` was past #83; `open-work.md` still called #20 and #21 the open work. A document that has
to be rewritten to stay true gets rewritten once and then never again — a log that is only ever
appended to does not have that failure mode. What is still *ahead* is not here: that is
[whats-next.md](whats-next.md), and it is forward-looking on purpose.

## Adding an entry

One row per merged pull request, at the top of the table, before the pull request is merged:

| Date | Issue | PR | What was decided |
|---|---|---|---|
| 2026-07-31 | #315 | #405 | **A board configuration is copied by everyone who clones the tree, and this one named an account.** The tracked `board.config.json` carried `repo: vitorengers/vibemaxxing` and the maintainer’s project URL; `ensureWorkspaceConfig` returns early when a config already exists, so a stranger who registered a clone of the release — the path the README’s own fork section leads to — got a board mirroring somebody else’s GitHub project, running `gh` against it and marking every card on it undraggable. **The file stays**, because `check-board-docs.mjs` and `check-board-map.mjs` read `board` and `docsDir` out of it; what left are the two fields that name an account. **`repo` is now written at registration from the project’s own `origin`**, through the `originRepo()` that was already in the tree — `git remote get-url origin`, not `gh repo view`, so nothing rests on an external CLI’s JSON shape the issue itself had not verified. No remote, or one that is not GitHub, writes no `repo` key rather than a guess. **`githubProject` gets no derivation and never will**: a project board belongs to an account, nothing on disk implies one, and a guess there points `gh` at somebody else’s board. **The overlay is `board.config.local.json`**, gitignored, beside the shared file and winning over it — the issue offered this or the settings dialog and preferred this, and its second reason is the decisive one: the dialog writes that file, so a dialog writing into a git-tracked config dirties the working tree of the project being edited. **The decision the issue put on this run was where a saved setting lands, and the answer is: back in the file it was read from.** `GET .../config` returns the two merged, so the dialog shows what is in force; a project with no overlay behaves exactly as before. It matters because the dialog sends every field on every save, changed or not — writing everything to the shared file leaves the edit shadowed and saving appears to do nothing, and writing everything to the overlay copies the whole config into a file nobody shares, so the project’s own settings stop reaching the machine. **The tree’s record of which repository it is moved to `FORK_REPO` in `scripts/lib/repo-identity.mjs`, and deliberately not into `package.json`.** Four checks read it — `check-readme`, `check-attribution`, `check-fork-identity` rule 2, `check-install-paths`’s `publishesOwnPackage` — and every one of them asks whether the manifest describes *this* tree or the one it was forked from. The state they were written for is a tree carrying upstream’s manifest verbatim, in which `name` and `repository.url` agree with each other perfectly and are both upstream’s; anchored on the manifest, `publishesOwnPackage` is true by definition and waves through the exact command it exists to catch. `check-board-map.mjs` already keeps `FORK_BASE` for that reason. **41 tracked files named the project URL** — the config, three prose files and 38 check scripts writing throwaway `githubProject` fixtures against a stubbed `gh`. Swept at byte level over `git ls-files`, owner segment only, to the neutral `someone` that `check-board-notes-column.mjs` already used; keeping the project number means no stub matching on `/projects/5` changed behaviour. Two literals went red honestly and were fixed: `parseProjectUrl` was expected to answer `login: 'vitorengers'`, and `check-project-card-limit.mjs` drew its cards from this repository’s own workspace, which now has no project board — its sections 2, 3 and 5 draw from a throwaway project configured to the same 8, and section 1 still asserts this repository’s own file. `vitorengers/vibemaxxing` as a `repo` fixture was left alone: a repository name is not a project board, and naming the repository the checks belong to is what a fixture there is for. **Both checks were written first and run red**, 11 cases and 2, against a copy of the tracked tree that registered carrying the maintainer’s project. **The board card was measured in a real Excalidraw** rather than estimated — seeded into a throwaway workspace and read back through `getSceneElements()`, settling at 661.1 against a card edge of 720. Structure map: the Workspace registry card names the overlay and where `repo` comes from, a file the loader reads being the boundary. **The risky first run is covered**: `board.config.local.json` was written into the development checkout before the merge, inert until this lands. |
| 2026-07-31 | #306 | #416 | **The shortest thing a new user could type was the one thing that looked like a hang.** Zero arguments routed to the MCP stdio server, which attaches a JSON-RPC transport to the terminal and then calls `process.stdin.resume()`: no output, no URL, no timeout. A bare invocation now resolves the port, spawns the server, waits for `/health` to carry the service marker, opens the browser and prints one line — and both outcomes of `ensureCanvasRunning` end that way, so a launch against a board already up brings the tab forward instead of reporting `spawned: false`. **The issue's own premise had expired, and following it would have broken what it set out to protect.** It asks for a third bin beside the two legacy names, branching on `argv[1]`'s basename so existing MCP client configurations keep working byte for byte. But #297 retired both legacy names at the rename, so `vibemaxxing` is now the *first* bin and the name every published `mcpServers` block uses — `npx -y @vitorengers/vibemaxxing`, which npx resolves to a symlink carrying the product's own name. The name alone would have handed every Claude Desktop, Cursor, Codex and OpenCode user a process that prints one line and exits. **So the rule is two conditions and both must hold: one of this package's own command names, *and* a person on the other end of stdin.** Stdin is what actually separates the two callers — a client always hands it a pipe, a person always has a terminal — and stdin rather than stdout because stdout is what a person redirects: `vibemaxxing > log.txt` is still somebody typing, and only the browser stays shut for it. **The Windows shim was measured rather than assumed**, which is what forced the second condition: a two-bin fixture package was really installed with npm here, and `cmd-shim` writes `"%_prog%" "%dp0%\..\<pkg>\dist\bin.js" %*`, so on Windows the name is gone before Node starts and no care with basenames recovers it. There the terminal is all there is to go on. On POSIX the shim is a symlink and the name survives. **A name this package does not install is the stdio server whatever is on stdin**, derived from "not one of ours" rather than from a list, so the retired names are covered without being written in `src/` at all — which `check-bin-identity.mjs` forbids anyway. **The opener lives in the launch command, not inside `ensureCanvasRunning`**: every canvas-driving command auto-starts through that function, so putting it there would open a window on `vibemaxxing describe` and then need suppressing again at each caller. `cmd /c start ""` on win32 with the empty title argument asserted — without it `start` reads the URL as a window title and opens nothing — `open` on darwin, `xdg-open` elsewhere, every failure degrading to the printed URL rather than to an error, and `VIBEMAXXING_OPEN_COMMAND` as the escape hatch for a machine that has none. `check-launch-command.mjs` was **written first and run red against the old tree on 16 cases**, "it never exited" among them, which is the defect as a person meets it; section 4 and the piped cases were green before and after, which is the half that must not move. The real opener was exercised against a local HTTP server with no override set, not only the recording stand-in. **This landed a day late and on a main that had moved**, because the run that wrote it ended its turn while `npm test` was still in the background — the incident #415 was built from, one row down. Merging current `main` conflicted in three documents, all of them both-sides-added: the CLI table's Server row is now the union and the count is **22**, being what that table lists rather than either side's number; `running.md` keeps both sections. The CLI card wanted five lines in a box sized for four and could not grow, an arrow starting at y175 against a box ending at y170, so `it starts the canvas as needed` was dropped as the least specific of the five to what either change did. **`npm test` is 200 passed and 3 failed, and the three are not this change**: `check-alt-arrow-accelerator.mjs` fails identically on `main` at c8c31e0, because it drives a visible Chrome through `SendInput` and needs a desktop session it can foreground, which an agent's shell does not give it. Its own header says it should skip in that case and that it is deliberately not part of the automated set, and the runner selects it regardless — so the suite is red on this machine whatever the change is. Left alone here on purpose; it is somebody else's row. |
| 2026-07-31 | #410 | #415 | **The prompt already forbade this, in these words, and both agents did it anyway** — *"you wait for every helper to come back before you do"*. #306 and #314 each ended their turn while a background `npm test` was still running, having put the wait itself in the background and believing something would call them back; in headless `claude -p` the turn ending is the process ending, so nothing did. Both exited **zero**, one with the change committed and never pushed and one with a pull request opened and never merged. So what this adds is a mechanism rather than a third copy of the rule. **A run that exits cleanly without landing anything, and that got somewhere, is re-entered once.** **A loop inside `runImplementation` rather than a second call into it**, which is the decision the rest follows from: re-entering would move the card to In Progress again, cut the worktree again, reset the start time, and have to get past the cap and the per-issue guard — a second door into starting a run, which is exactly what `beginImplement`'s comment exists to prevent. In the loop the slot is already held, the state stays `running` for whoever is watching, and the checkout survives between the two attempts because the release moved to after both. That last point is not incidental: `releaseWorktreeFor` removes a worktree with nothing *uncommitted* in it even when it holds commits the base branch has never seen, so a gate read after the release would have found #306's shape gone. **Exactly once, ever, and the bound lives on the record.** `carriedImplement` carries `recovered` forward the way it carries the start time — losing it would not merely forget something, it would grant a second recovery — and it is written *before* the second attempt starts, because a bound recorded on the way out is one a crash in between hands back. **Three refusals, and each of them is a different thing that would otherwise turn one automatic attempt into damage.** A non-zero exit is a broken command or a broken machine and a second identical attempt does not improve it; `blocked` from #409 is an agent that stopped for a person on purpose, and sending another at it would force the merge the first refused; and a run with no pull request and nothing in its checkout has nothing to *finish*, so re-entering would be a plain re-run — which is precisely what `dispatchQueue` refuses on purpose with *"the queue tries each issue once"*. That third gate is what keeps this inside the existing rule instead of quietly overturning it, and it is the reason the check builds a real git repository. **The escalation guard does not depend on the first agent having remembered its own signal**: the recovery prompt tells the second agent to look at the pull request before merging anything and to print `NEEDS A PERSON` itself if that is what it finds. **`unfinishedSection` is deliberately not `resumeSection`.** That one addresses an agent picking up a stranger's checkout after a server died, so it says none of this was reviewed, read it before you trust it; here the previous attempt was this same board minutes ago working from this same prompt, so the work is unfinished rather than suspect and telling an agent to consider discarding it would invite it to redo a change that is already correct. The text also differs on what is missing — with a pull request already open the only thing left is the merge, and an agent not told that opens a second one, which nothing downstream counts. **`AgentRun.code` exists because `ok` folds two questions into one** — exited zero *and* printed a URL — and the distinction between a turn that ended early and a command that blew up is the whole trigger; null for a timeout, a failed spawn and an interactive session a person closed, none of which a retry improves. **And the failure a person reads is now what the agent said**: `agentOutcome` reported the last 600 characters of stdout, which for a streaming agent is machinery, which is why #306's block explained itself with a `task_notification` and a session-end record. `lastThingSaid` returns the last assistant message and falls back to the tail whenever there is none — exactly right for a plain `claude -p` that prints prose, for which the tail was always the correct answer. `check-implement-recovery.mjs` was written first and ran **red on 11 assertions**, with the three no-recovery cases green before and after. It counts agent starts rather than inferring them — the stub writes the prompt it was handed to `runs/<issue>-<n>.txt`, so the files *are* the attempts — and reads the second attempt's prompt back to assert it was told the pull request already exists. **Not photographed, and said rather than glossed**: the card's data was confirmed in the file, in `/api/elements` and in the scene inside a headless Chrome, and the fit was settled by Excalidraw's own measurement — 456×146 of text at (178, 738) inside a box at (160, 690) 560×206 — but the throwaway screenshot tool could not frame it, its viewport arithmetic losing to the board's own fit, so this revision of the card was not looked at as pixels. The same card one line shorter was, on #411. |
| 2026-07-31 | #409 | #411 | **`done` was a fact about the agent's stdout rather than about the repository**, and on the morning this was written it was wrong for two runs out of eight: PR #403 (#314) and PR #405 (#315) were both recorded as successes while both pull requests stayed open, both issues stayed open and both cards sat in In Progress — the board reporting a finished run and work in flight for the same issue at the same time. The server now reads the pull request back through `gh` and lets the answer decide the record. **A `gh` that cannot answer leaves the outcome exactly where it was.** A blip, an expired login and a machine with no CLI all arrive as `null`, and a verification that turned any of them into a failure would be worse than no verification at all — it would invent defeats for runs that won. `fetchPullLanding` never throws for the same reason: every caller's answer to every failure is identical, and a caller that had to `catch` would be one missing `try` away from the thing this exists to guard. **The URL now survives a run that did not land**; the old failure branch wrote `url: null`, so a board that had just been told where the pull request was threw it away. **`blocked` is a fifth state and it exists because the prompt already sanctioned the ending it names** — leave the pull request open, say which files disagree, and stop for a person. From outside, that agent and one that stopped mid-sentence are indistinguishable: both exit zero having printed a URL for an open pull request. Recording both as failures slanders the first, and anything reading this signal that retries would then be automating over a human escalation, forcing the merge the agent refused on purpose. So the prompt asks for `NEEDS A PERSON: <url>` and the marker is searched for anywhere in the output, the way the URL itself is, because a streaming agent's stdout is `stream-json` and nothing it says is ever the last line. Case-sensitive on purpose: a false positive costs a run its automatic recovery, which is the direction that asks a human rather than the direction that acts. **Five independent enumerations of the same strings had to learn the new one** — `ImplementState`, `PanelRunState`, `asRunState`, `CardImplementState` and `SETTLED_IMPLEMENT_STATES` — and not one of them would have errored had it been missed, because `frontend/` is never type-checked and none of the `src/core` copies is an exhaustive switch. `CachedImplement` had already fallen a state behind through that same gap and now imports `PanelRunState` instead of restating it. **`EXCALIDRAW_GH_COMMAND` is sealed in `canvasEnvironment` rather than merely cleared**, which is the trap that module's own comment already describes for `.env` and the operator's real `claude.exe`, one door along: cleared leaves it unset, and unset is whatever `gh` is on the operator's PATH — logged in as them, against a fabricated `.../pull/99`, from checks whose headers say nothing here talks to GitHub. `lib/gh-unstubbed.mjs` answers `pr view` with `{}`, a well-formed reply that says nothing and therefore reads as "nobody could say": it keeps some thirty existing assertions green *honestly*, since a check that has not said whether a pull request merged has not said it, and it costs no retries. Everything else exits 1 naming the variable. **Deliberately under the legacy prefix**, because since #311 `fromLayer` prefers `VIBEMAXXING_`, so a default written under the new spelling would outrank the seven checks that stub the old one and silently replace their fixtures. `check-implement-merged.mjs` was written first and ran **red on 8 assertions** against the old tree — `done` for an open pull request, `done` for a closed-unmerged one, no `blocked`, and `gh` never asked at all — with three green before and after: a merged pull request is still `done`, a `gh` that gives up still leaves the run alone, and a run that produced no URL still asks GitHub nothing. `check-implement-in-progress.mjs` case 8 asserted "gh was never run" for a board with no project, which was true and overreaching — the question there is whether a workspace with no project touches a project — so it now asserts the board was never read and no card was moved. **The board card was looked at in a headless browser rather than diffed**, since the board is drawn to a canvas and `frontend/` is not type-checked, so neither would have said a word about eight lines in a box sized for six. **Stated because it is a second behaviour change and should not arrive as a surprise**: `beginImplement` refuses a second run with 409 when a record is `done` and has a URL, so a run that is no longer called `done` becomes startable again — by the button, by Resume and by the queue. That is the intent, and it is what #410 is built on. |

| 2026-07-31 | #316 | #408 | **The issue block — the feature this tool is built around — was unreachable until a GitHub project was configured.** `refreshProjectBoard` read the 404 a project-less workspace gets from `/api/project-board` as `clearMirror()`, and the notes column and its `+` are drawn by the mirror: no column, no `+`, and `addIssueBlockToColumn` returning with a `console.warn` nobody has open. Registration writes a `board.config.json` with a `name` and never a `githubProject`, so that was every newly registered project. **The canvas's own column is separated from the GitHub mirror**: a 404 draws `notesOnlyBoard()` — a `ProjectBoard` of *no sections at all*, marked `noProject` — and `layoutMirror` puts the notes column in front of it exactly as it does for a project of four, so one code path decides that column's geometry whether or not anything stands beside it. That is the answer to the issue's Risk; the alternative was a second placement rule for a region one column wide, and a layout change compiles perfectly either way. **A mark on the board rather than an empty-string test somewhere else**, because the canvas asks "is a board up?" in two places that mean different things by it: the cold-board strip #254 draws is skipped on a warm mirror so a blip cannot wipe a region somebody is reading, and a board with no project counted as warm would put #252's silence back on the next project that is configured *and* unreadable. The mirrored state — cards, move errors, the queue — is cleared **once**, on the pass that finds the project gone, because `renderMirror` skips a pass whose layout has not changed and a `signature` reset every twenty seconds would fight the reader's selection on a timer. **The run refuses, not the `+`.** Writing the observation down is the part that has to work before GitHub is connected; turning it into an issue is not, and the agent is told to create it *with `gh` in this repository*. `POST /api/issue-block/:id` answers 400 **before the spawn** when the workspace names neither a `repo` nor a GitHub `origin` remote — resolved in that order, the way `interruptedRuns` already resolves it, because a checkout with a GitHub remote has already said where its issues go — and writes the reason onto the block as `issueError` rather than only into a response the panel forgets. `check-notes-column-without-project-browser.mjs` drives Chrome against a project configured with nothing at two zooms, presses the `+`, asserts the draft reaches the **store**, presses **Research and create the issue** and asserts the refusal is on the screen with no agent spawned, then restarts the server and asserts the block came back — which is the issue's third *done when*, true already for a registered workspace and not waiting on #314. **Reconciled with #317 and #322**, which landed while this was being written: a 404 is still the one answer that *says* nothing — that is #317's contract and it is untouched — and saying nothing is not drawing nothing, so `check-github-status-browser.mjs`'s case 6 now asks that nothing *mirrored* is drawn beside the canvas's own column; and the refusal names an `origin` that is not on `github.com` rather than reporting it as no remote at all, through `originRemote` and `GITHUB_HOST`. |
| 2026-07-31 | #317 | #407 | **Every GitHub failure ended in the same silence.** `runGh` rejects with the last 300 characters of `gh`’s stderr, `GET /api/project-board` turns that into a 502, and `refreshProjectBoard` dropped it — so a missing CLI, a logged-out one, a token without the `project` scope and a typo in a project URL were one blank corner of canvas, repainted every twenty seconds. #254 had fixed the **cold** half, where nothing is drawn yet and a red strip goes up; this is the **warm** one, where a mirror that is already up and then stops being read said nothing at all, on purpose, because a blip must not wipe a region somebody is reading. **The toast is what says it either way** — `sayOnCanvas` carrying the server’s own sentence — and **the suppression rule landed with it rather than after it**: once per distinct message per board, cleared by a read that succeeds, so a board that recovers and breaks again speaks afresh and a poll that finds the same failure does not. It is keyed on the *server’s* sentence rather than the one shown, which is what keeps the diagnosis off the repeat path: an unchanged failure is dropped before anything is fetched. **`GET /api/github-status` is the diagnosis** — `gh --version` and `gh auth status` per workspace behind the memo the issue reads already use, answering `{ installed, authenticated, login, scopes, version, resolved, error }` with `error` the raw first line `gh` printed. Loopback only, because the login and the token’s scopes are in it; `/health` carries the same probe run once at startup for the *host’s* `gh` and carries only `resolved` and a version number, which is `agent-preflight`’s rule 2 applied rather than a second policy invented. **`installed` means the spawn succeeded, not that the exit code was zero** — a CLI that is installed but broken is a different complaint from a missing one. **The whole `gh` command is probed, not argv[0]**, parting company with the agent preflight on purpose: `EXCALIDRAW_GH_COMMAND` is a prefix every call already appends a subcommand to, where an agent command carries the operator’s permission flags, and probing argv[0] would report `node` — which always exists — for a wrapper that does not work. **And a 404 stopped meaning two things.** It covered both “this board has no project” and “the URL in the config is not parseable”, and only the first should be silent: most boards are the first, and a toast on every one of them would make the feature unusable, while the second is somebody who *tried* and got the silence meant for somebody who had not. `ProjectUrlUnparseable` is a **sibling** of `NoProjectConfigured` rather than a subclass — a subclass would pass an `instanceof` test in a caller written before it existed and be answered 404 again, which is the silence being fixed — and the route answers 422 `bad-project-url`. The issue asked for `GET /api/health`; the route is `GET /health`, and the field went on the one that exists. `scripts/check-github-status-browser.mjs` counts the toast off `console.warn` rather than the DOM, because Excalidraw’s toast lives ten seconds and a second one with the same words changes nothing in the DOM to see — the DOM watcher is what proves it rendered. Run against the build without the fix first: 17 red, and green on the two that must not change. **#318 landed in the middle of this one and fixes the same symptom from the other end** — it widens what parses and refuses a bad value as it is *typed*; this refuses one as it is *read*, which is the config edited by hand and the config written before that check existed. Neither is the other’s fallback, and the refusal sentence moved to `project-board-types.ts` so both ends say the same thing and a classic repository project is named as one in both. **#311 landed too**, which makes `env()` the only place `process.env.EXCALIDRAW_*` is spelled in `src/`, so the preflight reads `GH_COMMAND` through the accessor — not a style point here but a correctness one, since the command may come from `config.json` or a `.env` and a preflight that read one layer would answer about a command the runs do not use. `GH_STATUS_MEMO_MS` is declared with the rest. |

The board waited on longest goes cold past the cap, which costs that board nothing but the reconnect it used to pay every single time. **The second — does a background board autosync — is answered *front board only*, and it falls out rather than being enforced**: autosync pushes the *scene*, and a background board has none on screen. It does not poll either, for the same reason the poll was already gated on `document.visibilityState`: nobody is reading that mirror. **A board mid-switch is not kept**, and that guard is the same crossing #156 was about one layer down — the socket already names the board being entered while the canvas still draws the one before it, so a copy taken then would file one project's shapes under another. That board is closed as it always was: one switch paying the old price beats a warm board that is wrong. **A background board that loses its socket goes cold rather than reconnecting.** There is nobody behind it to be told, and a retry loop per board would be several reconnect schedules running for boards nobody is looking at; it gets its own `ping`/`pong` instead, because a half-open socket reads OPEN until TCP gives up and here nothing else would ever notice — a board that came back showing an hour-old scene it had stopped being told about would be worse than one that reconnects. **One server change, and it is what keeps this from costing something**: `clientsWatching` is how an export or a viewport request for a board with no browser on it is refused at once instead of waiting out its thirty-second timeout, and a socket held open for a board nobody is looking at would have answered for it. A socket now says which of the two it is — `{ type: "watching", active }` alongside the existing `ping` — and one that never says is watching, which is every other client. `scripts/check-warm-board-browser.mjs` is new and was run against the old build first, red on exactly three cases and green on the two guards beside them. It wraps `WebSocket` and `fetch` before the bundle loads and counts both, because *the element is on the canvas* passes against the old code too — one full reconnect later, which is the complaint. With no socket opened and no board pulled, the only way what was created while the board was in the background can be on screen is that the board's own socket delivered it. The pill is watched with a `MutationObserver` rather than sampled: against a local server the old reconnect could be over in a few milliseconds, and a poll that missed it would have passed for the wrong reason. **Not investigated, and still not**: whether a board should keep its selection and its open panel across a switch. The panel is still cleared on the way out, deliberately — an issue card left open over another project's board is the reason that line exists. Structure map: the *Canvas in the browser* card gains the line, since a tab that stays live is a feature of the frontend; `docs/canvas-frontend.md` has the section. |

The constructor therefore ignores the binding whenever `input` is set rather than leaving the trap for a caller, and `mode` says `pipe` so no block claims otherwise. That settles the issue's blocking open question the other way round from how it was posed: because the process inside the tab **is** the process that ran before, in the same checkout, reading the same prompt, there is no repainting screen to scrape — one `agentOutcome` decides `done` with a pull request and `failed` with an error for a watched run and an unwatched one alike. The price is a tab that is read-only in substance, and `write()` swallows a keystroke rather than echoing one that would read as though the agent had heard it. The operator's `EXCALIDRAW_IMPLEMENT_AGENT` is not rewritten — no flag, no appended argument — which is the rule `agent-usage.ts` already keeps. Two more answered: #122 had already landed as #139, so the marker question was moot; and the tab **does not come to the front**, because four unattended runs at the default concurrency, plus a queue that starts them with nobody watching, would take the view away from a shell somebody is typing in. `POST /api/terminal` gains an optional `command` and `cwd` so the server's own entrance is one a person can also reach — a facility only the server can use is one nothing can check by hand — with all three guards untouched, since a shell is already a thing you type commands and `cd` into. `check-implement-terminal.mjs` went 14 cases red against the old code and caught something on the way: a board running on this machine exports `EXCALIDRAW_TERMINAL=1`, so the case about a board with **no** terminal was quietly being run against one that had it. Structure map: the Terminal card says a run opens one in its worktree. Left for its own issue and written into `whats-next.md`: the **issue** agent has the same gap and the seam already exists — `runIssueAgent` simply does not pass a `host`. |

- **Date** — ISO 8601, the day it lands.
- **Issue** — the issue the branch was cut for, or `—` for the handful of early changes that
  went straight to a pull request.
- **PR** — the pull request. It is written before the merge, so the log runs one entry ahead of
  `git log`; `scripts/check-board-map.mjs` only fails the other way round, on a merge with no
  entry.
- **What was decided** — the decision, not the diff. `git log` already has the diff.

A change that also moves the architecture — a file, a route, a block kind or a feature added or
removed — updates the **Project structure** map on the board as well. `CLAUDE.md` is where that
rule lives.

## Entries

| Date | Issue | PR | What was decided |
|---|---|---|---|

Ids reach everywhere the old code said "the session": the routes, where the id is optional but its absence is never *guessed* at — one session is unambiguous, several is a 400 that lists them — and the socket, where a new `terminal_sessions` message replays every live session on connect as a whole set, so a viewer reconciles rather than only adds. One emulator per session, all kept alive, because rebuilding on a tab switch replays a transcript into a fresh parser and a full-screen program left in a background tab comes back as its own scrollback. Which tab is in which block is deliberately not saved — the blocks are derived — so a reload puts every live session back into one. Reconciled with #98 and #103, which landed mid-flight: the erase restore became the reconcile, per-session geometry and all, and the font size feeds the grid for every block. The tombstones now go *around* the conversion rather than through it, which is both what keeps an erase erased and what keeps a deletion reaching the store. |

## The pattern under the first eleven

Three of the first eleven issues were the same shape of bug: two writers to one store, with no
rule for what happens when they disagree. Clear-and-replace sync (#1), schemas that dropped
fields another path accepted (#2), and a global `Map` shared by every board (#8). The fix each
time was to make the rule explicit rather than to make one writer back off.

That is still the rule the newer half of this log follows. The mirror and the terminal are the
same problem read the other way: they have exactly one writer, so they are marked *derived* and
never stored at all.

## The habit that keeps costing

Every behaviour change here ships with a `scripts/check-*.mjs`, run against the old code first.
The ordering is not ceremony. Three defects in the UI layer — a panel that never opened, a race
in tab startup, a click landing on the label instead of the box — compiled, type-checked, and
did none of what they claimed. Nothing short of driving a real browser would have caught any of
them, which is why the browser checks exist and why `CLAUDE.md` says compiling is not working.
